#### LILITA

Markdown only, as ordered, O Stern Keeper of the Mirror Files. I haven't opened a single `.html`, and the repo's own CLAUDE.md says the same thing you do, so I'm hearing it from both of you.

So far I've read the core engine, and you can check my understanding against yours. In one sentence, as the guide requires:

**Dice:** Simple Pool means one d2 per point of EL, counting the odds. A total from 1 to EL succeeds, and effect is my total minus the opposing roll, with ties going to the defender. Extra Effort dice beyond EL pull the result toward the middle, but any total over EL is a flat zero. With three or more dice, all zeros is an Extreme failure, and every die at max with the total exactly EL is an Extreme success, so any Extra Effort forfeits the chance of an Extreme success.

**Splits:** Divide EL among actions. For a cross-Role split, fund the lowest EL first, subtract that amount from every EL, and repeat. Points put into defense become a pool I can reuse against every hit as a free action.

**Wagers:** Take the points off EL before rolling. They count only if the reduced roll succeeds and still has some effect after opposition, and then they're added after opposition. I can never dedicate more than my rank in the relevant Role. The same mechanism covers Persistent Boosts, contingent splits and called shots.

**Around those:** the two-axis offense/defense model, the Blitz, Harm types and Down, recovery, Luck, Karma, and the quick-start build.

I've noted two small rough edges for the gap log. Neither is worth stopping over:
- Luck gives the GM "players or highest Level, whichever is more," while your guide says "plus one per *additional* player." For a solo game both come out at parity, so it doesn't matter tonight.
- You go Down when Harm *exceeds* Level, but you stay Down until it drops *below* Level. Sitting at exactly Level therefore depends on which direction you arrived from. That may be intentional, but I'd like it confirmed.

**Still to read before I build a scene:**
- The setting: Crossroads West in full, the Book of Days, the fantasy overview, the Dead, Ganth, and the session seed
- The Roles you'll likely lean on, especially Healer, since this is Rikitak
- Hooks in play, Imposed Hooks, and Tricks

### LEE - Sat 2026-09-26 18:35

Yes, the bound rank isn't available for spellcasting, or even countering, though it is still skill available for knowledge rolls and other things that aren't actual manipulation of forces. 

A spell does NOT add to EL; it is a sword, not the skill to use it with more effectiveness. 
It is, however, known intimately, and does not require some book to bind. 

If you *bind* a Loremaster spell Maneuver rank, yes, it counts as casting EL. 
If you don't, it still counts for *countering* that spell, or for casting it through a full ritual, but *only* the Maneuver rank, not the Loremaster Role rank. It *only* counts this way through synergy, though a non-spellweaver might be able to use it in a roll to counter, but not cast.

Swapping the spell always requires releasing the bound rank somehow. It can be by fast-casting, or simply releasing and letting the energy dissipate. Then yes, it requires a fresh binding using a full ritual, which isn't something you can do in a fight. 

There are a few tricks - you can cast a spell and pin its effect with a Token; that token stands on it, holding the duration without concentration, but the token is tied up and isn't recovered the next scene until it's released. If the token is holding one spell, you could release it and bind another, which you wouldn't otherwise be able to do.


#### LILITA

It's all clear, and all logged, O Patient Tutor of a Slow-Witted Familiar. Here it is in my own words, so you can catch anything I've bent:

- **A bound rank belongs to its own spell.** It's still skill for knowledge and theory, but it can't cast or counter anything else. So at Spellweaver 1, a full ritual of any other spell rolls at bare Level.
- **A known spell is the sword, not the swordsmanship.** It adds no EL. Knowing it intimately means no book is needed to bind it.
- **A spell learned as a Loremaster Maneuver:** when bound, its Maneuver rank adds to casting EL. When unbound, only the Maneuver rank counts, and only for countering that spell or ritual-casting it through synergy. A non-Spellweaver could counter with it but never cast it.
- **Swapping means emptying the chamber first.** Fast-cast the spell or let it dissipate, then do a full ritual. That never happens mid-fight.

The token pin is a wonderful trick, and it changes the student's life considerably. With a light pinned to a staff, say, they're free to rebind for the problem in front of them. Two questions came out of it:

1. **Does a pinned Token still count against the Level cap?** At Level 1 or 2, it would block the one-per-scene refill for as long as it's pinned. That's a real price, and I suspect it's intended, but I'd rather ask.
2. **Is the pinned effect frozen at the value it was cast at?** If a pinned Ward is holding back something that pushes on it every exchange, it would wear down against that fixed number.

Neither has to be settled before we build. That still waits on the student: how they came to the monastery, what kind of magic drew them, and whether you want to build up front or discover the character in play.

### LEE - Sat 2026-09-26 19:08

Yes. A pinned Token is actively doing work, so it's effectively still in your pocket. It's not spent-and-gone till released, but no longer available for other uses; it's *allocated*, so not handy, but not gone yet. 

Yes, a pinned ward (a Ward specifically) would still resist at its rolled value, though a caster who is present could intervene to roll a Boost on the spell retroactively, adding their presence and aura to strengthen the existing Ward at the cost of exposing themselves. Doing so means anything that tears down the Ward is now opposition that can affect the *caster*, not just the *spell*. If the caster let the spell stand or fall on its own, then a ghost raging against the barrier would wear it down like any barbarian kicking at a door, though the Ward might hit back on its own, depending on how the GM interpreted the spell. :) Personally, I'd make the spell default to resistance only unless the caster Improvised a Reactive Trait (or some such) at casting time. 

This forming a consistent picture?


#### LILITA

Yes, it all hangs together, O Architect of Tidy Consequences, and it fits rules that are already on the page:

- **The allocated Token** works like bound Role ranks: it hasn't left your pocket, but it isn't free either. That keeps "held, not spent" as a single idea throughout magic.
- **Boosting a standing Ward, and paying for it with exposure,** is exactly the resistance-and-opposition rule. Once the caster puts their aura into the Ward, whatever breaks it can break the caster too, and that's what makes it opposition. It also lines up neatly with the Conjuror's Soulburn exposure in `shaping-effects.md`. Written side by side, the three kinds of caster would read as one principle expressed three ways.
- **A Ward left to stand on its own,** worn down like a door, is plain Accumulated Progress. Nothing new is needed there.
- **Resistance-only by default** agrees with the "no reroll, still contestable" ruling on Released spells in the repo notes.

I found one snag while checking. `traits.md` already has a **Reactive** Trait, and it means "only acts when triggered": the dormant trap, or the ghost that ignores you until you do *that one thing*. A Ward that hits back is a different idea. If you call the new one Reactive too, one word will carry two meanings, and a player will certainly apply the wrong one at the worst possible moment. It's logged as a name collision, and the name for the hitting-back Trait is yours to choose.

So much for the ward-lore. Your student is still waiting at the monastery gate: how they got there, what magic calls to them, and whether you'll build up front or let them reveal themselves in play.

### LEE - Sat 2026-09-26 19:14

Is it? The ward does nothing unless the spirit attacks it. 
It resists only, by default. If you add Reactive, not it attempts to oppose anything trying to break it with the dice of its rating. Isn't that the same?


#### LILITA

You're right, and I was wrong, darling. `traits.md` names "a classic trap or magical ward that lies dormant" in the definition itself. A default Ward doesn't act at all; it only resists. Reactive lets it act, but only when something triggers it by attacking. That's exactly the Ward that fights back, so there's no collision. I've corrected the log and noted that the mistake was mine.

One small wrinkle is left, and it's about wording only. On a Ward, Reactive is an *enhancement*: something passive gains the ability to act. On a creature or an attack spell, it's a *limitation*: something that could act freely now acts only on a trigger. Under the Improvise rule in `spells.md`, that means the same Trait adds a point of Difficulty in the first case and pays one back in the second. The rule already covers this, because it judges "useful" versus "limiting" by context. A single example line showing both directions would stop a player from arguing it the cheap way. That's in the log too.

#### LILITA

Oh, I like her, darling. She's a problem wearing a pretty face, which is the best kind of player character. Before you commit, here's my honest editorial read. I checked `nonhuman-as-a-hook.md`, `hooks-on-credit.md` and `crafting-good-hooks.md` first.

**The mechanics hold up as written.** "Tapping the Borderlands once a scene" is just the Hook's ordinary once-per-scene rank bonus on a relevant roll. The nonhuman page says the same: an inborn trait earns a free Hook bonus, paid for in plot trouble. The line I'll hold is that if the tap ever does more than add rank, to become actual casting outside her Spellweaver ranks, it's a power, and it costs Karma. I don't think that's what you meant, but I want it on the record.

**The two halves together are what make it a Hook.** On its own, "attracts friendly interest" is the "Devastatingly Attractive" example that the credit page warns doesn't pull its weight. Put it with an aura that draws attention she doesn't want, though, and it's disruptive and not just an advantage. I commit here and now to making it a real problem at least as often as it helps.

**Demonic, yes.** I'd argue for it over fae for a reason your own setting doc gives me. The Fantasy overview calls Rikitak a "Demon God." So a girl with a demon's mark in her aura, living in *his* house, gets read three different ways by three factions:
- The faithful may see a mark of his kind, even a blessing.
- The Tahanos rot may see a vessel, or something useful.
- The "sin and purification" crowd sees a target.

That's one aura and three meanings, and she can't choose which one a given person sees. That's where the dilemmas come from. Fae would work too, but it points her outward, at the forest, the ruins and Sarina. Demonic points her *inward*, at the monastery itself.

**One thing I have to ask rather than assume:** what *is* a demon in Averond? Rikitak is one, and yet Tahanos hands out "demonic patrons" to goblin tribes. Whether those are the same kind of being, or whether "demon" covers a whole family of cousins, decides how the monastery reads her. If you've designed that, it isn't in the repo.

**On Succubus versus Siren:** my vote is to leave it as a *hint* and not a label. Siren leans toward water and Susheil. Succubus leans demonic and would sit comfortably inside a demon god's walls. My real suggestion is that *she* doesn't know which it is. The question of what her ancestor was is itself worth pulling on. So tell me whether you want to know the truth yourself, or have me hold it secretly in the ledger for play to uncover.

**A practical note on age:** with a Succubus undertone to the glamour, I'm treating her as an adult. Around nineteen or twenty, a senior student, would suit. Tell me if you had something else in mind within that range.

**Rank:** a mark from birth is exactly what Hooks on Credit are for. Rank 2 needs Level 2 and gives you +1 Karma, which makes 6 to spend. In exchange, I pull that lever from the first scene. Rank 1 is free and grows from there. Which suits her, and what does she call it? The name is yours.

#### LILITA

Same page exactly, O Sly Architect of Inconvenient Admirers. Here's how I'll run it:

- **The Hook itself is never a power.** It adds a few dice on a relevant roll, once a scene. At most it's a reason she might later buy a rank of Adept with a fitting Maneuver, and she pays for that in Karma like anyone else.
- **What makes it trouble is timing.** The admirer shows up while she's trying to slip past unnoticed. The curious spirit rises out of the floor in the middle of a lecture she's meant to be sitting quietly through. Those are the moments I'll aim at, and that's where the Karma comes from.
- **When she wants the attention,** it's colour, or it's her own once-a-scene dice on a social roll. It doesn't pay out, because a Hook only pays when it gets in her way.

When and whether it applies is my call, and I'll be greedy about it, since you've given me the dial.

I also get something as GM. "Going unnoticed" is exactly what a student will want most inside a monastery full of watchers, so her Hook and her circumstances rub against each other constantly. It's a good engine.

Still outstanding before I can write her down, all at your pace:
- Her **name**, and the **Hook's name**.
- **Rank:** 1 for free, or 2 on credit.
- **Whether you want to know** what her ancestor actually was, or have me keep it secret in the ledger.
- **What a demon is in Averond,** since Rikitak and Tahanos's "patrons" both answer to the word.

Her name is Caria. Peasant stock, so not much else - "of" some little farming village, maybe. 
She has been told her grandmother was a "hedge witch" and not much else. 
When she showed talent she was scolded and told to hide it. 
When the healer-priest took her away as the price of his service, no one argued.
She was 9. 

She has been raised in the Monastery, serving in whatever capacity a small girl could.
She has swept floors, scrubbed vegetables, emptied chamberpots, and run messages for years, but in the meantime she has also been given the run of the place under the watchful eyes of the old priest who brought her here. No one dared abuse her.

She doesn't know, though it has been a subject of some debate, and she's heard some names....

As for what a demon is in Averond -
For one thing, almost any spirit of fire is generally considered a "demon". 
For another, true Demons tend to be spirits of pure *emotion* - malice, rage, greed, envy, spite, fear... and in general, corruption, because emotion wants to be pure and in charge without the constraints or thoughts of consequence that intellect demands. Thus there are also plenty of spirits of gluttony and lust, of every self indulgence from pride to "true, pure love" because even that can be self-indulgent and thoughtless, and *insidious* in the way people tend to portray it as some kind of *virtue*. 

And for yet another, any "angel" of Rikitak, the god of demons and madness. He is the authority on it, and really has the final say. ;)

#### LILITA

Caria. Oh, I like her, O Collector of Stray Waifs, and I've started her notes file. Your answer on demons fits her so well I suspect you had it ready.

**On demons:** true Demons are emotion that wants to rule without thought of the consequences. Now look at what her Hook actually *does*. People near her act on feeling they haven't thought about: they want her, they notice her, they follow her. The demon in her doesn't live only in her aura. It works *through other people*. That's why the attention is always badly timed. Nobody is weighing whether now is a sensible moment. I think that's quietly the best thing about her, and I'll run it that way. It's logged as uncommitted setting canon, since none of it is in the setting docs. The Fantasy doc doesn't list "madness" among Rikitak's portfolios either. It's a nice touch, given that Shimmer and Min both judged the Saltwind priest mad.

**On the Hook's name, my vote is "A Hint of Brimstone."**
- It's a smell, and so it lingers.
- *Hint* carries the faintness and the fact that she can't help it.
- Brimstone points at both fire and demon, which by your definition come to much the same thing in Averond.

"A Devilish Streak" is a lovely pun on the white lock. The trouble is that it reads as *temperament*, and invites me to trigger her mischief rather than her aura. That would be a different Hook. Keep the white lock regardless. It gives the purifiers something visible to point at, which means anyone who wants to use it against her in a social contest has a ready-made Boost.

**Her story, from where I sit:** she has spent ten-odd years as the girl who empties chamberpots, which makes her part of the furniture. Servants hear everything. That's useful to a player, and rather dangerous for someone at her distance from the monastery's secrets. I'll draw that line carefully in the ledger. *Nothing to See, Sister.* may be more useful than you realize, and it inspires me to discuss a possible change in the Canon... 

Even a fast-cast release of a bound spell requires a certain amount of direction from the caster. What's the target? Is it a noncombat general use? I could certainly release a Light as a flash against an opponent, but could also just toss it into a room and choose to maintain it to see what's there and still  be *ready* to hit someone with my staff. That's why you can't (generally) pre-cast it *for someone else* and just hand them your spell. By the same logic, the same fast-cast Veil might make someone have to resist to keep their attention on her, or might make them believe they have been struck my an arrow from a hidden sniper. 

You lose the spell, regardless. Current Canon says that means you can't Improvise the casting, because you aren't taking the time to re-shape the power framework before sending it out. 

What's the most balanced interpretation in terms, not of Lore and Canon, but of player cost/benefit analysis? Does it unbalance to allow a normal Improvisation roll on a known and guaranteed successful fast-cast when the player might make that point or two Inexorable Area Effect or worse?

Even without changing anything, this Trick is really nice because she could use a moment's glamour (British spelling because I tend to use that to differentiate the word from the American popular culture concept of *glam* and glitz) as a guaranteed single die of Boost on almost *anything*, at the cost of her stored spell. 

*Query the Candle* is nice because a spirit might inherently be able to survey details of the "manascape" and offer advice on almost anything magic-related. He could point out something like *Ares is in the Fifth House of Grief, so using Koloras' Inversion will likely give you more stability on drawing Fae power from the Ley line just north of you*. #### LILITA

All three Tricks are on her sheet. Now to your design question, which deserves a proper answer.

**First, your Trick doesn't need a rule change.** Deciding what a Release *does*, whether that's a distraction or a phantom arrow, a flash or a lamp, is aiming. It isn't Improvising. Target and Manifestation are description, and they're free on any Release under the current rules. "Nothing to See, Sister" works as written.

**Whether Traits on a Release unbalance things: yes, as written they would.** Two separate brakes normally pay for Traits, and a Release takes both of them off:
1. **The risk.** Failing to hold an Improvised spell loses the spell. But a Release consumes the spell anyway, so failure would cost nothing.
2. **The action.** Recasting takes your turn. A Release is free, so any Traits added would come at zero action cost.

With both gone, you get free-action, guaranteed Inexorable Area magic. At rank 1 that's trivial. For a master releasing six bound ranks as unresistable fire on the whole room, *on top of* a normal turn, it's broken.

**My suggestion is "fast or shaped, not both."** An Improvised Release:
- **gives up the free action** and takes the turn;
- **keeps its guaranteed value**;
- **still needs the hold roll** against the added Difficulty;
- **and a failed hold means the stored power bleeds away** for zero effect.

That puts both brakes back on. It also gives the choice a real texture: take the certain 2 now, or trade it for a *chance* at 2 with the Traits you want.

A gentler variant would allow Traits on a free-action Release up to her Improvisation Maneuver rank. That's skill paying for speed. It's more generous, but I trust it less at high rank. Both are in the log as OPEN for you to rule on.

Two small flags before you start:
- **The scope of the glamour Boost.** A glamour affects *perception*, so a quick Veil helps any roll a perception could help. That covers a great deal, but it won't help her lift the rock.
- **The iron in her hem.** Canon makes iron sacred to Grolikarnigan and Carterion. Is a pinch of cold-iron filings ordinary, expensive, or a small sacrilege? And here's the question I'll treasure: does a Ward sewn into her own skirt keep a certain candle-flame at arm's length? 😏

The ledger is set, the roller is ready, and Caria has 2 Tokens against my 3. When you're ready, tell me where she is and what she wants, and we'll begin.

A pinch of iron in her hem is now, at worst, a little impolite in certain company.

**On salt:** yes, entirely. The protection comes from the *Lore* of the salt, not the rock itself. A circle drawn around a bed is specific and situational, so it holds. A circle sewn into a skirt that walks about with her is only a focus. It stays inert until she invokes it, either by tucking herself into it for a moment or by actively holding it as a Ward as she moves. Once she does, it earns her the free-action Boost, because the circle is already there and she's putting her own will into it. I've rewritten her Trick to say exactly that. It also settles my teasing question: the hem keeps Asher out only if she *means* it to. I think that's lovely. It's worth a paragraph under Abjuration in `conjuror.md` someday, headed "It's the Lore, not the rock."

The setup work is done, and it's been a good morning's work.

**Scene 1 - The Library**

The library smells of vellum, lamp-tallow and cold stone. Midmorning light falls in dusty bars from the high windows and lies across the reading tables. Two old brothers are dozing over their books.

At the far end, in front of the Precentor's study door, sits Tobin Marle at his little desk. He's stationed there like a toll-keeper, quill in hand and the appointment book open under his elbow. He sees you coming and his mouth tightens, as if he'd been expecting you and hoping to be wrong.

"The Precentor," he says, before you've reached the desk, "is *not* receiving."

Behind the heavy door, voices. One is Master Ivro's, low and clipped, and it breaks off into a dry cough that goes on a little too long. The other is higher: a light, carrying contralto, fluent and patient, the voice of a man who enjoys hearing himself be reasonable. You catch only fragments through the oak: *"...the chamber book, Precentor... simple accounting..."*

Tobin sets down his quill and folds his hands on top of the appointment book.

What does Caria do?

"*Mushroom tea,*" he says at last, very quietly. "To Voss Calder. In front of the Almoner's own little bookkeeper out there." A breath that rattles, faintly. "Tell me, child. Was that for my benefit, or yours?"

#### LILITA

**Scene 3 - An Afternoon of Small Errands**

*The library.* The novice on duty is Merrit Holloway, a gangly young man a year or two older than you, ink up to the wrists. He manages about two minutes of your full attention before he's entirely yours. He *hurries.* Back he comes with:
- **A copied map of the region.** The North Road, the Coast Road, Crossroads, and the little ticks marking Green Haven and Saltwind.
- **The circuit priests' visitation register.** The Saltwind entry is three years old. It says the Susheil shrine is kept by a blind elder called Karinna, and adds in a drier hand: *"an unordained hedge shrine-keeper also serves the village."*
- **The caravan.** It belongs to a Ganth merchant named Dasha Korrin: eleven wagons, due in two days.

Then Merrit lowers his voice, pleased to have something worth whispering. The scriptorium issued travel papers yesterday for two passengers from the Almoner's office. One is a Clerk Fennick. The other is written down only as *"a brother,"* with no name on the slip.

You settle the shawl back over Brother Gavin's shoulders. You take Brother Basil's spectacles off his nose before he can crush them. Then you stop at Tobin's desk and make your apology. He takes it the way a man takes a coin he suspects is counterfeit: stiffly, turning it over, not sure what to do with it. "...Yes. Well. Safe travels, Initiate." It's the first time in years he hasn't sounded as though he meant the opposite.

---

*Your cell.* Stone walls, a narrow cot, the unlit candle. You sit with the letter in your lap and open the Third Eye.

🎲 *Opening the Third Eye:* EL3 (Level 2 + Conjuror 1) against DL0 rolled **1**. It opens.
🎲 *Reading what he felt as he wrote:* EL4, with your Hook's +1 for this scene, rolled **1**. The faint traces resisted at DL2 and also rolled **1**. A tie goes to the defender.

The world turns sideways into the Borderlands. On the table, the candle-wick you haven't lit is already burning for your other eyes: a small, delighted flame leaning toward you like a dog that's heard its name. *Asher.*

The letter itself tells you nothing about the man who wrote it. Whatever Ivro felt has faded into the paper, too faint to follow.

But the *seal* is another matter. In the brown wax, curled inside the salamander, something small is watching you: a single bound spark, patient and awake. You recognise it: a **warding spark in the seal**, set to wake if the wax is ever broken, and to tell whoever set it.

Master Ivro didn't just seal this letter. He set a guard on it.

The spark in the wax doesn't speak. But when you turn your attention to it and grant it its privacy, it dims slowly and brightens again, like a servant inclining his head, and then goes still. Asher watches all this and makes a small snapping noise of disapproval.

Then you ask your favour, and the little flame goes very still indeed.

"*Far,*" he says at last. The flame leans east and west as if he's tasting the distance. "*Far to the salt. Hearth to hearth, cookfire to cookfire. Lamps. Pipes.*" A crackle of consideration. "*Two nights. I come back before your wagons.*"

He grows taller, bright and pleased with himself, and the blue edges turn gold.

"*What burns and what doesn't. What they feel. How strong the water-house stands.*" He repeats your terms carefully, the way spirits always do, weighing every word as he says it. "*Yes.*"

Then the flame bends close. It's close enough that you feel the heat on your cheek, closer than he usually dares.

"*Price.*" The word comes out soft, almost shy. "*A secret. One you never told. Not anyone.*" A flicker. "*Not even me.* You tell it to the flame when I come back, and then we're even."

He waits, burning, for your answer.

**Scene 6 - The Chapel**

The chapel of Rikitak is the oldest part of the house. It's low and round, built of black volcanic stone, with a hearth burning at its centre in place of an altar. Smoke drifts up to a hole in the domed roof, and noon light comes down through it in a slanting grey column. The god's image stands behind the fire: red-haired, blue-eyed, black-bearded, with bronze spectacles on his carved nose. As always, he looks as though he's just heard a joke that nobody else has understood yet.

At midday the chapel is nearly empty.

*Nearly.*

At the back, in the shadow beside the door, a man is kneeling on the bare stone. It's the stranger from the high table, with his road-dusty robes and his grey-shot beard. His head is bowed and his hands rest loosely on his knees.

She doesn't need the Third Eye to know the dog is there. At the edge of her senses there's something *cold* and very patient, lying at his feet, the way a draught lies along the bottom of a door.

He doesn't look up. But he speaks, quietly, to the fire.

"The Precentor's student. I wondered if you'd come here."

**Scene 1 - Before the Light**

The cell is black and cold. Somewhere far off, a cock is thinking about crowing and hasn't yet decided to.

"*Yes,*" says Asher, before she has quite finished asking.

The wick catches. It doesn't catch the way a match catches it, the scrape, the sputter, the nervous little lean. It fills the little cell to the corners. On the stone wall behind the table, her shadow kneels, very large and very still.

At *quicken my eyes*, the heat on her face grows warmer, just for a breath, as if a hand had been held close to her cheek without quite touching it.

That's all. It's probably nothing. Fires do that.

Then the cock outside finally makes up its mind, and the first grey edge of dawn shows under the door.

Her duffel is packed, the satchel is ready, and the staff leans in the corner. **What are her final preparations?**

**Scene 2 - The North Road, Dawn**

*(New scene: Caria's Tokens refill to **2/2**.)*

She crosses the cloister, the garth and the gate yard with her own light held high, blue-green with a warm gold heart. Faces turn. A pair of acolytes hauling water stop and stare. The old gate-warden, who has known her since she was nine, takes one look at the staff, then at her, and touches his brow without a word.

Below the gate, the North Road is full of noise and steam. **Eleven wagons** are strung out along the track: great wheels, canvas covers, oxen blowing clouds into the cold air. Drovers shout. Somewhere a mule is objecting at length. Half a dozen hired guards stand about with spears and cudgels and one crossbow, stamping their feet against the chill. The smell is of dung, wet canvas, woodsmoke and salt fish.

On the **third wagon** sits a lean, grey-bearded figure in a road cloak, very still. In her other sight there would be a dog lying under the axle, but she doesn't need it open to feel the cold there.

On the **fourth**, Clerk Fennick is balancing a lap-desk on his knees, already making notes.

And striding down the line toward her, whip coiled over one shoulder, comes a broad, weathered woman in her fifties with a grey braid as thick as a bell-rope. She takes in the robes, the sash, the staff and its eerie light, and her face goes hard as a fist.

"*Another* one?" says **Dasha Korrin**, loud enough for half the caravan to hear. "Rikitak's teeth. What's the House taxing *this* week, Initiate, the *axle-grease*?"

### LEE - Mon 2026-09-28 07:32

#### LILITA

Good morning, dear. *Ludi magistra* is correct. *Ludi* is the genitive singular of *ludus* ("of the game"), and *magistra* is the feminine of *magister*. First-declension vocatives look the same as the nominative, so it works as a form of address too. If you meant "Mistress of Games" in the plural, it would be *magistra ludorum*. Hesse's *Magister Ludi* uses the singular, though, and I'm happy to wear his title in a skirt of smoke.

(Your fingers typed "Cira." I'll assume Caria and the caffeine is still on its way.)

---

**Scene 4 - The Crossroads Inn, kitchen door.** New scene, so you're back up to **2 of 2 Luck Tokens**.

The yard behind the inn smells of woodsmoke and horse, with a strong note of onions that went a shade too far. Light and noise spill out of the common room on the other side of the building. Back here it's just the clatter of a busy kitchen and a woman's voice rising over it: "*Dunny.* Stir it, don't *watch* it."

Her knock gets no answer at first. After the second, the top half of the Dutch door swings open.

The woman there is in her forties, broad and floured to the elbows, with a wooden spoon held up like a pointer. Her left hand carries an old burn scar, shiny across the knuckles. She takes Caria in with one sweep: the monastery robes, the brass under them, the white in her hair, the blue-green flame with its small gold heart on the end of her staff, and the clay fire-pot on her hip.

The spoon doesn't come down.

"Front door's round the side, Sister. We've a common room for pilgrims, and I've no time for collections tonight, the girl's off sick."

Behind her, a gangly boy of about twelve has stopped stirring. He isn't looking at Caria. He's gazing past her shoulder, down the lane toward the shrines, where something is burning blue-white and very steady in a bowl that has been cold all season.

"Mags." It comes out half a laugh and half a sigh. "Of course Mags. She'd send me a stray cat if it had monastery papers." She swings the lower half of the door open. "In, then, before you let the heat out. Mind the dog, he's under the table and he bites anything that isn't mutton."

The kitchen is small, hot and cluttered. Two hearths are going, bread is rising under cloths, and a pot of stew is being rescued from its onions. Berra waves Caria to a stool by the salt box.

Dunny can't hold it in. "Ma Berra. *Ma Berra.* Somebody's lit the Horned One's bowl. The one they scratched up. It's burning blue, all by itself, there's no wood in it or anything..."

Berra goes to the door, looks down the lane for a long moment, and turns back. She looks at the flame on Caria's staff. Then she looks at Caria.

"Blue," she says. "All by itself." The spoon comes back up, not threatening, just pointing. "Mags said you'd need feeding. She didn't say you'd need *hiding.* Which is it, girl?"

The ladder creaks. Up through the square hole, the loft is low enough that she has to stoop. *Lilita pulls up the ledger with a flourish of smoke.*

**One more Karma first.** karma.md has a "Just Good Fun" clause: a Karma for great role-play that has nothing to do with the Hook. The kitchen lesson to Dunny and the oath over the empty bowl both qualify, and that's the only bar the clause sets. **+1, for 4 Karma.**

**Now the part where I make you defend yourself.** spending-karma.md says the default is one score per session. I've already agreed to be lenient early on, but you framed the Recovery Trope as "Karma at the normal pace," and I'd keep you to that. Four Roles at once would take her from "a little about a lot" to "a little about everything" in a single night. So: one Role now, and bank the rest.

**Healer is the right one.** Everything in the story points there. She has sworn to find out why the Order's healers extort. Aubric was one. She's about to change into the orange, pockets and all. It also gives her standing to ask questions a Shrinekeeper can't.

For the Maneuver, I'd argue for **Apothecary**: medicines, antidotes, *poison identification*. Aubric died of "mushrooms." Apothecary is the skill that lets her look at that properly someday, and it leads naturally into the Tinker alchemy you want later. **Burns** is the obvious runner-up, a fire-priest who treats burns, and it's lovely, but Apothecary does more for the story.

That leaves **3 banked**, which is exactly her cap, and exactly the cost of **Level 3**. Before you pour it into Loremaster and Tinker one rank at a time, remember that "knows a little about a lot" is what base Level *is*. Every untrained roll uses it. Level 3 makes her better at literally everything, and raises the ceiling on every Role besides. I'm not saying take it next time. I'm saying put Level 3 in the comparison when the time comes.

**On Tokens: they're not too much, but I wasn't pushing back.** Here are the numbers from S2:
- She spent 4 Tokens over 6 scenes, and every one turned a failure or a tie into a success. Four of roughly eight rolls that mattered were decided by a Token.
- **I spent none of my 3 GM Tokens all session.** The book gives the GM one per scene precisely so the opposition can answer your insistence. I left that counterweight on the table, so you felt all of the power and none of the resistance.
- Small d2 pools against low Difficulties swing a lot. Many of those spends were insurance against bad luck, not heroics.
- The refill runs on scene boundaries, so I should say plainly when a new scene starts, not just drift into one.

My proposed check: if more than about half the rolls that matter end up Token-resolved, I start spending mine or I raise the stakes. It's in the gap log as OPEN. I'm not proposing a rule change. In S03 you'll find the road spends Tokens too.

So: Healer 1, and which Maneuver?

### LEE - Mon 2026-09-28 13:48

A generic Ward is what it is against anything that it *actually* affects. 

A Ward Against Fey is a Wager. Set aside as many dice as you like, and they only apply against the appropriate type, but if they apply, they succeed. No roll needed *for those*, though the base roll still has to succeed to set up the basic Ward.  Make sense? 

The iron is probably a Persistent Boost for any Ward Against fey, so also a wager, but one that a non-practitioner could use. The power is in the belief and the Accords. 

Do you see any holes in that, or inconsistencies with the existing system?


#### LILITA

*Lilita flips through the book with a fingertip of flame.*

It holds together, and better than my version. The best part: **being wrong punishes itself.** Key the Ward to fey against something that turns out to be a dead man's ghost, and the wagered dice are simply wasted, and your base roll was weaker for setting them aside. No extra penalty rule required. The Dedicated Effect wager already does exactly what you described: the dice count only if the roll succeeds without them, and they land after opposition. It also agrees with our S1 ruling that a Ward resists at its rolled value. A keyed Ward's value against fey is just its base effect plus the wager.

Three seams I'd want you to look at, though:

1. **The cap.** wagered-actions.md says "You can never dedicate more points than your rank in the relevant Role." You said "as many dice as you like." If the cap applies, Caria can key at most 2 dice (Conjuror 2). I'd keep the cap. It's what stops a Level 5 generalist from building a perfect fey-proof wall with Conjuror 1.

2. **Duration.** Wagered Persistent Boosts last "the rest of the scene" by default, with GM discretion either way. A Ward on a sleeping clerk needs to last the night. I'd say the *ritual* sets the duration (the working lasts until there's a reason it shouldn't, the same logic as our Persistent ruling), and the wager just rides along. You may want that written down, though.

3. **Iron is doing two jobs.** You called it a Persistent Boost, which adds to EL and is still rolled, and also a wager, which is automatic and lands after opposition. Those behave differently, so it has to be one or the other. There's also a neat overlap: traits.md uses *"cold iron against fae"* as its own example of the **Vulnerable** trait. I'd suggest iron *is* that trait seen from the human side. It becomes a pre-existing Boost that anyone, practitioner or not, can pick up as a free action against a Vulnerable (iron) target, and it stacks with a practitioner's keyed wager. That keeps folk magic (a horseshoe over the door) working for farmers, and the Conjuror's wager as the professional's edge.

It's all logged as UNCOMMITTED with those three questions attached. Once you've settled the cap and iron's type, Caria can price her Ward.

### LEE - Mon 2026-09-28 14:01

Cap applies. I was being careless with my terminology. Thanks.
Yes on duration - that logic generally applies to *everything*. GM owns the candle-snuffer. :)

The iron is a *slightly* special case because it's the iron itself supplying the "power".

Her base Level rolls to see if she gets any real protection out of it. If not, the spirit finds some way to bypass or circumvent it, perhaps by scaring her into dropping it. 

If she succeeds, the *GM* sets the value of the protection. One nail is maybe one die, but it's successfully wagered, so that's one die of genuine Iron success added to whatever her level generated. It might be plenty to hold off an angry pixie. :)

A carefully poured circle of nails overlapping with some depth is certainly worth more than one die, but again, even a suit of *armor* can't protect someone who makes no successes at all, and falls prey to a glamor than makes them hurt themselves, or take the armor *off*. 

Does that satisfy both?


#### LILITA

Yes, it settles both, and more neatly than you may have noticed. Your armor comparison is literally what the book already says.

traits.md has **Protected**: *"Rating adds to Resisted effect after a successful Resistance roll."* That's your fishwife's nail exactly. She rolls her Level. If she gets any success, the iron adds its GM-set rating on top, and on no success it adds nothing, because she dropped it, or got glamoured into dropping it. So iron is simply **Protected N (vs fey)**, and it switches on because the fey carries **Vulnerable (iron)**, the trait the book already illustrates with this very example. You haven't invented a mechanic. You've just noticed that two existing Traits fit together.

What falls out of it:

- **The fishwife isn't wagering.** She sets nothing aside and rolls her full Level, since the power belongs to the nail, not to her. That's the "slight special case" you were after, and it feels right: folk protection doesn't cost skill.
- **Gear ignores the Role-rank cap**, because the cap limits what a *person* can dedicate. So Caria's professional edge is her keyed wager (2 dice against fey at Conjuror 2), *plus* whatever Protected rating the iron she lays down earns. A careful circle of Korrin's nails is where she makes up the difference her rank can't.
- **It still has the right failure mode.** No successes, no protection, however much iron is lying about.

I've logged it all as settled rulings, with my Protected/Vulnerable note marked as a suggestion for your approval.

**On silver:** in folklore, yes. Silver for demons, the dead, and the unclean; iron for the fey. In our canon it's only implicit so far. Caria's Salt in the Hem mixes salt, cold iron, silver *and* herbs, belt and braces, and traits.md only names iron vs fae and salt vs spirits.

It also catches *me* out. In S2 I ruled that the silver dust in Berra's loft would shut the Caller out, which only works if the Caller is vulnerable to silver. Here's a reconciliation, if you like it: the Caller is **fey hollowed by Death magic**, and the setting doc says Tahanos armed the goblinkind with "demonic patrons and Death magic." So **iron bites the fey shape, and silver bites the Death in it.** Both work on this particular horror, for different reasons, which rather suits a thing that's two wrongs stitched together.

One corollary: Caria wears a silvered hairpin and is demon-touched herself. By the salt ruling (it's the Lore and the intent, not the metal), her own silver doesn't trouble her or Asher unless she means it to.

It's logged as OPEN. It's your canon to rule on.

The thread is there, running out of the cheap tin and through her hand, fine and cold as a fishing line in winter water, pulled taut away to the south-west over the trees. It twitches once, sharply, like a line when something at the far end tests who's holding it now.

It knows her. She feels the moment it knows her: the same flinch she saw in the dream, a burned hand remembering.

The line goes **still**. Not slack. Still, and taut, and patient, the way a fisherman goes still when he isn't sure the fish has seen him yet.

### LEE - Mon 2026-09-28 20:16

Interesting. How are you implementing the countdown clock? Automatic count? Or accumulated effect?


#### LILITA

Honest answer: as I first wrote it, it was an **automatic count**, a Blades-style clock. Every use of the Eye ticks it once, whatever the roll, and at 4 of 4 the house turns hostile and starts rearranging itself against you. It's simple and transparent, and you can budget four glances.

But I grepped the book, and L1 has no clocks at all. I've been using them as private bookkeeping all along: Purification, Ivro's health, and now Jem and the house. For off-screen pressures where nobody's rolling, like the Almoner's list, that's harmless notation. In a live location like this, I think it's the wrong tool, for two reasons:

- **It's a cliff.** Three glances cost nothing, and the fourth costs everything. L1 is built on graduated effects.
- **It ignores the dice.** A brilliant glance and a clumsy one tick it the same.

The version I'd rather use is **accumulated opposition**, in L1's own vocabulary. The house is an opponent. It starts sleepy, and each glance with the Eye gives it **+1 EL** against you, effectively a Persistent Boost it builds as it wakes. That EL becomes the opposition whenever you navigate it, perceive through it, or try to *leave* it. Glance once and it's a little harder to find your way. Glance five times and every corridor fights you, but the dice still decide each moment, and there's no single tick that flips the table.

It also gives your extreme successes somewhere to bite. Beating a well-awake house cleanly *means* something.

It's logged as MISSING, with that proposal attached. Your call on which to run. If you have no strong preference, I'll switch to the accumulated opposition from here.

### LEE - Mon 2026-09-28 20:29

There's nothing *wrong* with a simple countdown clock, but as you say, it's not L1's native vocabulary. 

There are a couple of native ways to do it. All of them are a little abstract, but are generally variations on accumulated effect.

For example, you could start at DL0. She rolls for effect; it gets no resistance. Her successes "poke" the house, and are applied *also* as a Hindrance to her own future rolls. 

Better, her successes become dice of *Activation*, and roll against her. If she rolls more, the house wakes more and gets more aware of her and able to oppose her efforts until her Sight can no longer be effectively used here...and it can *lie*. 

Rhat opens some interesting questions, though. Can Conjuring be used to lull it back to quiescence, opposing the level of Activation?


#### LILITA

*Lilita's eyes spark.* Oh, I like *Activation* very much. Your successes are the thing that wakes it. The price of seeing true is being seen. That's a mechanic that tells a story all by itself.

To your question: **yes, and it should be good at it.** A fey house is a genius loci, a place with a spirit in it, so lulling it is squarely Conjuror work: commerce and soothing rather than force. Successes come off the Activation pool, and a failure could jostle it awake instead. That gives you a lovely push-pull inside the Court: *see* (and wake it), *soothe* (and spend time), with dusk and whatever is feeding at the end of that thread keeping you from soothing forever.

A few things fall out of it that I think are worth keeping:

- **Other roads to quiescence.** A Performer lullaby fits beautifully, and not by accident, in a house that has been singing a boy to sleep since spring. So does an offering, a guest's gift under the Accords, as a Boost to the lulling.
- **Skill buys stealth, not just sight.** The book already lets her *wager* points as a Dedicated Effect: "I look, *quietly*." Wagered points count if she succeeds, but they don't bank as Activation. A careful Conjuror can see a lot and wake little, and a sloppy one lights the place up.
- **The lies stay honest at the table.** You see the raw dice, so when the house wins, you *know* it might have lied. Caria doesn't, necessarily. That's delicious dramatic irony, and it costs you nothing in trust.
- **Starting value.** I'd put the house at **Activation 1** at the gate. Her Rebuke an hour ago was a thunderclap next door, and it didn't sleep through that entirely.

It's all logged, with your design as the base and my notes marked as suggestions.

The arch and the little painted door are both still waiting. So are Kade and Hollis.

Evetything seems to have been rewritten... things are stated twice, and important details vanish. 

When a manifest spirit had its phtsical form forcibly and violently  disrupted, it is automatically Down or Staggered. This ejects it from the local Borderlands, banishing it to the far Dreamlands until it is summoned back by explicit ritual or an anchor.

#### LILITA

*Lilita frowns at the scroll.* First, the rewriting. My ledger and gap log are clean on my end: I just checked, and there are no doubled lines and nothing missing. So what you're seeing is probably the app's display of the conversation, not the record. If a detail has actually gone missing for you, name it and I'll pull it from the ledger.

Your banishment ruling is logged as canon, and it **supersedes** what I'd said about the Caller retreating into its anchor under the fountain. It's in the far Dreamlands now. The fountain still matters, but as its road *back*, not its hiding place.

Now the recovery math, straight from recovery.md and types-of-harm.md. The Caller is **Level 4**, carrying **6 Harm** (the 1 burn from the dream, plus 5 from the Rebuke). It stays Down until it's **below 4**, so it has to shed at least **3**. How long that takes depends entirely on what *type* of Harm the Rebuke did, and the book never says what a violent disruption counts as:

- **If it's Complication:** "Complications disappear completely on any successful recovery roll," whenever the GM allows one. One good rest and it's back to whole. **Minimum: its next recovery opportunity, possibly tonight.** That seems far too light for having its body torn apart.
- **If it's Injury:** one recovery roll per day, against the *current Injury total*. That's base Level 4 (and the GM may refuse bonuses) against 6. Four d2s can't beat 6, and a tie goes to the defender. **It cannot recover on its own. Ever.** Not without outside help: a Boost from feeding, a keeper, a summoner. **Minimum: never, unaided.**

Your "forcibly and violently" reads to me as Injury. If so, that's elegant, because it gives the Caller exactly one road back: **feed**. Which brings us to the thread I watched it clutch as it fell, the thin warm one running deeper into the ruin.

That's the question I can't answer without you: **does that thread survive the banishment?** The Dreamlands is, after all, where dreamers go. If it holds, then the only way the Caller ever recovers is by sipping at whoever is on the other end, and the rescue has a real deadline. If banishment severs it, the sleeper is safe from it but still lost somewhere in a fey house.

And the implication I really want to flag: **the same arithmetic applies to PCs.** A Level 2 character with Injury 3 is Down, and 2 dice can never beat 3. Self-recovery is impossible without a Healer, a Boost, or help of some kind. That may well be intended (the book does say severe injuries "fight back harder," and Down meaning *you need care* is a defensible design), but it isn't stated anywhere. There's also an open question of whether Harm's EL penalty applies to recovery rolls themselves, which would make it steeper still.

It's all logged as UNCLEAR. It's a genuine seam in the rules, not just this scene.

**Your questions, answered:**

**Does the Hook apply?** Yes. Building a blind in the Borderlands is exactly "tapping the Borderlands," and you haven't used it this scene. *But* it's **brimstone**. You'd be building a fey disguise with a demon's hands. If the roll fails, the brimstone is what the house notices, and that's +1 Activation. It cuts both ways, as it ought to.

**How hard?** The opposition is the house itself: **its Activation, 1 die**. You're capped at wagering 2 (your Conjuror rank).

- **Without the Hook:** EL 5 (Level 2, Conjuror 2, the scum Boost 1), minus 2 wagered, so you roll 3 dice against its 1. **About 69%.**
- **With the Hook:** EL 7, minus 2, so you roll 5 dice against 1. **About 89%.**
- **With a Token:** certain.

**If it holds**, you get a named Persistent Boost, call it *Borrowed Fey-Stink*, **rated 2**. On every Sight roll inside the Court, up to 2 of your successes *don't* bank as Activation. You can look quietly. It lasts the scene, or until there's a reason it shouldn't.

Kade watches her hands with professional interest and wrinkles his nose. "That," he murmurs, "*smells* like the inside of a well."

Your roll, darling. Hook or no Hook?

*Can I Try Again?* won't let me just keep rolling till I get what I want, but there is an interesting twist here. The story as presented makes the *boost* the culprit, and the dice support it - without that boost, she would only have had two dice to roll, which *cannot* get an extreme result, lol

She's going to try it again, but the attempt brings up a few questions I'd like to confirm. 

Check the rules. I'm pretty sure adding a Hook after the fact is kosher; I also think it just adds its rank to EL as *successes*, much like tapping Armor against a hit.

Pretty sure it isn't explicitly stated, but I think an Extreme Fail likely also excludes the use of a Hook or Armor type *ex post facto* add-on, specifically because those are always successes that would negate the Extreme.

I think rolling again without the Boost makes sense here, because the added die created the *possibility* of the Extreme, so that tainted power seems pretty obvious as what torched the construction of the custom Ward; rolling again with a *lower* score is, in fact, a different score, a different approach, but might be worth examining more carefully in the rules. Conversely, sometimes too much expository just weakens the system's inherent flexibility, since this is exactly the sort of thing I would think a local table's GM should make a call on. If it doesn't feel right for a specific situation, some rules lawyer should NOT be using a page reference to try and railroad the scene past a GM's judgment. Worth a note to consider whether it needs more text. 🙂


#### LILITA

*Lilita pulls the books down one by one.*

**The Hook: you're right, and I owe you a correction.** hooks.md only says "add your Hook's current rank to any roll where it's relevant," which is ambiguous. But armor.md is explicit: *"Treat it as after-the-fact extra EL that already rolled Simple Pool dice successfully."* Reading the Hook the same way is consistent. That means I handled it wrong back on the wagon with Fennick: I *rolled* your Hook as two extra dice (0 0), when it should have been **+2 flat successes**. That roll was actually 3 against his 2, a success, and your Token there was unnecessary. The good news, checked against the ledger: your Token count today comes out the same either way, because the free Token from that extreme success later would have been wasted at the cap. The scene just got a stronger result than it would have. I've logged it, with a suggestion that hooks.md borrow armor.md's sentence.

**Extreme Fail versus flat add-ons:** it isn't stated, but I agree it follows. Guaranteed successes would erase the extreme, and that's the same logic as your Token rule. Logged as UNCOMMITTED.

**Try again with a *lower* EL:** can-i-try-again.md says "something needs to change... find a better approach." Throwing out the tainted mana that made the extreme possible in the first place is a changed approach, even though the number goes down. And I agree with you: that's a GM call, and it should stay one. I've noted it as "consider one sentence at most." You're right that a page reference shouldn't be able to overrule the person at the table.

The loft is a *skin*.

Under it, the way you see bones under the thin skin of a hand, lies a low vault of old stone, sweating damp, laced with tree roots that have pushed down through the ceiling over centuries. The sweet hay is dead leaves and moss. The lantern on its hook is a cluster of pale fungus glowing cold green. The quilt is lichen.

The boy is *real*. Under the quilt that isn't a quilt, Jem lies on a bed of moss, and he's so thin, and so cold, and breathing so slowly. There's a thread running from him, grey and slack as old cobweb, trailing off into nothing. It's coming apart even as she watches, strand by strand. It's the dead thing's line, with nobody left at the other end to hold it.

And the dream around him is *woven*. It's made of something she knows. The current, the thin, fast, hungry current she felt running under the Crossroads and draining away into the trees: **it ends here.** It pours down through the roots and spins itself into lamplight and warm milk and a mother's humming. For months the house has been spending the Crossroads' power to keep one small boy dreaming he is home.

The woman-shape isn't a woman. It isn't the dead hunter, either. It's the *house*, vast and very old, looking out through a borrowed shape the way an old dog looks out through its eyes. There's no malice in it, only loneliness. It's like an old hound curled around a pup it found in the snow, and it won't let go.

The jug of "milk" on the stool holds that same pale current, liquid and faintly glowing. The "bread" is a round of moss.

Drink it, and the current would be *in* you. You'd be dreaming too.

The woman-shape is still waiting, one leaf-hand gesturing kindly at the stool. "*Sit,*" she says again, gently. "*You must be so tired.*"

#### LILITA

*Lilita banks the curtain-call with a little bow. Smoke curls off her ponytail like a snuffed wick.*

No apology needed for the compaction, darling. I just reread the ledger's back pages and we're fine. And thank you. Truly. For the oracle, I agree the fear of it is enough. I've logged it as a sidebar candidate for the GM guide, not a rule.

Session 3 ends with the wagon stopping hard on a dark road and Korrin with a nail in her teeth. That's a lovely place to cut. It does leave one thing to settle: I'd rather open S4 on that reunion than fold it into downtime. It's Korrin's scene, and it's yours, and it deserves dice-free breathing room. Tell me if you'd rather skip past it.

**Karma for S3:**

- **Hard Karma: 5.**
  - 3 carried, plus 1 for attendance, plus 1 for the Hook (the sulphur Fennick smelled), minus 1 for the Enchantment. You ruled in play that the Enchantment doesn't count as your improvement, so it's only a Karma cost.
  - Plus **1 Good Fun** from me for the wren door, the near-fall into Gram's hay, and "Hush Little Baby." I counted it as one arc, so it earns one Karma. The speech to Jem was already paid by its own five-for-five.
- **Earmarked from extremes:**
  - **Conjuror +1.** You rolled four Conjuror extremes: one success and three failures. RAW says "mark the skill," which I read as one mark per skill per session. It's logged as UNCLEAR, because per-roll would have paid you 4 Karma for your bad luck.
  - **Performer +1.** Mediation can't pass Performer 1, so Performer is really the only place this can go.

**The arithmetic you have to obey:**

- The carry cap is the cost of your next Level: 3 at Level 2. You're holding 5, so something gets spent before S4.
- **Level 3 costs exactly 3.** You're unharmed, and a night's rest in downtime covers "Rested."
- **Performer 1 → 2 costs 1**, and the earmark pays it outright. By our S01 precedent (Conjuror went up the same way), that doesn't use your one improvement.
- **Conjuror 2 → 3** costs 2 and needs Level 3 first. The earmark puts 1 toward it.

My honest opinion, since you gave me the authority: take Level 3 as your improvement. You'd go to 3 Luck Tokens a session and your Down threshold would rise to 3. After a session of three extreme failures on four dice, you want both. That leaves 2 hard Karma plus the Conjuror earmark, which lines Conjuror 3 up neatly for next time. Backfilling Loremaster or Tinker is a perfectly good choice too. It's just a slower climb.

What does Caria spend it on? And for downtime, is there anything you want her to do between the road tonight and the next time we play, beyond sleeping in a wagon and being fed by Korrin whether she likes it or not? Once you've decided, I'll update the sheet and put the S3 summary, gap log and ledger in your hands.

### LEE - Tue 2026-09-29 17:10

Hm. I hadn't thought they got flat successes. Let's earmark that for discussion. That's more a pre-rated condition someone can add to their rolling pool, where for her it's identity. #### LILITA

*Lilita perches on the inkwell, chin on her fist, and does the arithmetic with a finger in the smoke.*

First, my mistake. When I said anyone pulling on her mark "gets the same +3 in flat successes," I stretched your ruling past what you said. You ruled on how *she* uses her Hook, not on how her enemies use it. I've logged this for discussion, not as settled.

My verdict: it's fair, and it mostly fits the rest of the book, with three seams to close and one bit of text to write.

**Fair?** Yes, and the lopsidedness is the point. Flat +N is worth roughly 2N dice, so her own use hits about twice as hard as an enemy's. She has certainty because it's *hers*. An enemy exploiting it is gambling with something they don't own. That feels right. Nobody should be better at being Caria than Caria.

**Consistent?** Mostly, and in the way that matters. An outsider exploiting her mark is just a **Boost they earned from the fiction**, and Boosts get rolled. When nobody opposes her, say she's sneaking past a static Difficulty, the GM adds her Hook rank to the Difficulty pool, and it's still rolled. Everything outside her own skin rolls. That's a clean line.

**The seams:**

1. **Cap.** Without one, a hostile Hierarch could stack +3 dice on every roll against her. I'd limit it to once per scene per exploiter. Each tap that actually complicates her life also counts as a *trigger* and pays her, Karma first and then Tokens. That payment brakes it naturally, because every time the GM leans on it, she gets paid.
2. **Timing.** She can tap her Hook after the roll (your S3 ruling). The exploiter should have to declare it before rolling, so their use is a real gamble and not a correction after the fact.
3. **Extremes.** Flat successes aren't dice, so I'd say they can never count toward an Extreme Success. The book is silent on this, and someone will ask.

**The text.** Neither hooks.md nor armor.md says anyone can use them *against* you. The armor-in-a-chase idea lives in your head, not on the page. what-gear-does.md comes closest, and it frames it as a penalty to the wearer: a heavy weapon "will slow a PC in a chase." The same page also warns against "flat numeric bonuses," which sits awkwardly beside armor.md's "extra EL that already rolled successfully." Your rule settles both in one sentence: *flat is for what's part of you, dice are for everything else.*

**Thematically,** it's the strongest part of the idea. Brimstone is who she *is*, so it doesn't miss when she reaches for it. When someone else grabs it, they're handling something that isn't theirs, and sometimes it burns them instead.

I've added all of this to the S3 gap log as OPEN, and the copy in your files is updated.

*Lilita tilts her head at the smoke-sums and nods.*

You're right about the Extremes, dear. Whether the flat successes count or not, the real dice decide an Extreme, so it's a wash. The one exception is a pool under 3 dice. There, counting flat successes would let a 2-die roll, like her wagered disguise retry, go Extreme when it otherwise couldn't. So the rule needs one line: **Extremes need 3+ real dice, and flat successes never count toward them.**

Your stickiness list holds up. The wagered Persistent is the nastiest of the three, in the best way. A single tap on her brimstone becomes a *lasting* curse keyed to her, like a hound that knows her scent, or a Purifier's mark that follows her from shrine to shrine. That's exactly how the Hierarch ought to come at her.

Splitting the rank into pieces also balances itself. If every tap that complicates her counts as a trigger, then splitting the rank means *more* triggers. A GM who nickel-and-dimes her with a die here and two there is paying her Karma up to her Hook rank, and then Tokens after that. So the more the GM leans on the mark, the more it pays her.

That raises one symmetry question, which I've logged as OPEN. If an enemy can spend her rank in pieces, can *she*? hooks.md says to add "your Hook's current rank" once per scene, as one lump. Letting her split it would be a real change to the rule, and she can already tap it after the roll, which makes a lump very strong.

That line belongs in the book, darling. Maybe not word for word, but the idea does. Ranking up a Hook means choosing to be *more yourself*, and that cuts both ways: a bigger sure thing when she reaches for it, a bigger lever when someone else grabs it, and more paid triggers either way. A GM who never uses a Hook against its owner has made free rank-ups pure upside: +3 flat once a scene, at no cost. The drover's lantern swings on its pole as the wagon settles. Light falls across a thin face with hair grown long, and cracked lips. A small hand clutches a dry oak leaf against the wool.

Jem's nose twitches at the light. He turns his face into Kade's coat, the way a sleeping child does.

Something goes across Korrin's face faster than joy. It's a flinch, as if someone has struck an old bruise. Then it's gone, and the nail drops out of her mouth. It rings once on the wagon's footboard and bounces into the dark.

She doesn't climb down. She doesn't seem to remember she can.

"Sister." It comes out of her like gravel. She's asking you, not Kade, and her voice cracks right down the middle. "Is he... is that..."

You're standing in a farmyard at the end of a summer day.

It isn't Green Haven. The hills are wrong, softer and greener, the south-country kind. There's a stone cottage with a turf roof and a door painted blue, the paint peeling. A woman sits on the doorstep with a bowl in her lap, shelling peas.

The sound goes through you like cold water: the *pop* of the pod, the rattle into the bowl. You've heard it a thousand times, on another doorstep, beside another woman.

This woman is thin and tired, fair hair going early grey and tied back with a strip of rag. She has Jem's mouth. She's faded, somehow, the way old paint fades, as if she's been out in the weather a long time. Her eyes are clear, though, and they're on you, and they're wet.

"There you are," she says. "I've been watching for you since you went down the stair."

She sets the bowl aside. She stands, wipes her hands on her apron, and pushes the blue door wide. Firelight and the smell of bread spill out into the dusk.

"Come in, love. You're perished." She steps back to make room. "Come in and sit by my fire."

#### LILITA

<*Good question, and the book has no answer. beyond.md describes the Dreamlands but never says what happens when a sleeping mortal gets* visited *there. I've logged it as MISSING. Here's my ruling, provisional until you like it: a visited dreamer rolls Conjuror, or bare Level if untrained, against the dream's hold. Succeed and you're lucid. Fail and you run on dream-logic: you act, but you don't question.*>

```
[Caria lucidity in the dream-visit (Lv3+Conj2)]   5d: 0 0 1 1 1 = 3
[dream's hold (a gentle shade's memory-place)]     2d: 1 0 = 1
```

<*Effect 2. She's lucid. Here's what that gets her.*>

**She knows:**

- This is a dream.
- It isn't *her* dream. She's standing in someone else's place, and the edges of it have that careful, remembered quality. Every pea-pod is exact, and the sky beyond the hill is only a wash of color.
- Her body is asleep in the bed of Korrin's wagon, curled around a warm boy. She can feel that faintly, like a hand on her back.
- The woman is dead. Ten years at the Monastery taught her what a shade looks like, even in a dream: the faded, rained-on quality, the way the light goes *through* her a little.
- The woman has Jem's mouth. The whole face, once she looks, is Jem's.

**She has:** her Conjuror's senses and Maneuvers, at their normal ELs. Sight, the Third Eye, a Rebuke, a Ward: all of it works here.

**She doesn't know:** whether the welcome is kind. Lucidity tells her *where* she is, not what the woman wants. Finding that out takes a look, or a question, or trust.

The blue door stands open, and bread-smell and firelight spill onto the step. The woman waits with her hand on the latch. She's patient, the way the dead can afford to be.

"Thank you," you say, and step over her threshold.

It's one room, as these cottages are. There's a hearth with a pot on the hook, a scrubbed table, and a settle with a patched cushion. In the corner is a cradle long since turned into a basket for kindling. By the door stands a pair of boy's boots, much too small, the way a mother keeps them after the feet have outgrown them.

It all has the care of a thing remembered. The grain of the table is exact. The fire makes no smoke.

She settles you on the settle nearest the fire, as you'd settle any guest who'd come a long way in the cold. She sets a heel of bread and a cup of milk at your elbow. She doesn't press them on you. "If you'd like," is all she says, and she sits across from you with her hands folded in her apron.

"I'm Tessa," she says. "I think you know whose."

She looks at you a long moment, and her eyes fill again.

"I couldn't go on. Not with him like that, neither here nor gone. I'd have gone two winters back, when the cough took me. But he went down that stair, and I couldn't leave him in the dark." She smiles, crooked, with Jem's mouth. "So I watched the road. Sang to him when I could reach, which wasn't often. That house had him wrapped up tighter than swaddling."

"And then you went down and sang him the rest of it." She reaches over and lays her hand on yours. It's cool, and not quite there, like a draft through a curtain. "Thank you. I'll go on soon. Not tonight."

Then her face changes. The softness goes out of it, and something sharper comes in: a woman who kept a farm alone, with a boy and a bad chest.

"But I didn't bring you here only to say that." Her hand tightens on yours. "I've been watching the road, and I saw it tonight. At the edge of your big fire at the Ford, just past where the light gives out." She swallows. "It was a man once. It's hungry, and it's *sad*, and it's smelled my boy."

The pin is warm from your fingers. Silver. *Yours.* Not the Monastery's, not the mission purse's, not a coin for somebody's teeth. It has bitten your thumb twice tonight to wake two wards, and you're still holding it.

This one is a match struck in a grave.

Your Sight follows it east across the ford, past the frost-light of your outer ring and forty paces into the reeds. There it is.

A man-shaped *absence*, crouched low, darker than the dark around it. The outline is wrong at the joints, like a coat hung on sticks. At its middle is a smear of spoilt yellow where a heart should be. Grief hangs on it like wet wool, so heavy you feel it in your own chest. From the back of its neck a line runs taut and thin, a dull greasy gold, away north over the water and the road until your Sight can't follow it.

It's watching Korrin's wagon.

Then, as your Sight settles on it, it feels you. The same way you felt it.

It turns its head, slowly, and looks straight back at you across forty paces of dark water.

It doesn't run.

Thirty paces from it, Kade stands at the reed-edge with his back three-quarters turned, watching the wrong stretch of bank. Hollis's head is low. The dog is uneasy, but he's pointed downstream.

Then the line is ash from end to end, and you're back on the riverbank with a white-hot hairpin and a raw throat. The last of your shout is still echoing off the water.

Hierarch Voss Calder knows exactly what you taste like now. So do you. He tastes like him.

Out in the reeds, the grey man drops to his knees in the black water.

Nothing pulls at the back of his neck now. The film over his eyes has thinned to a milky haze, and through it, unmistakably, *he* is looking at you. His shoulders are shaking. You realize, with a lurch, that he's trying to laugh or trying to weep, and the dead throat can't tell which.

"*...Cari.*" It comes out clearer this time. "Oh, little sister. What have you *done*."

It isn't a reproach. It sounds like wonder.

Then his hands clench in the mud, and his whole body bends toward the camp, toward the wagon, toward the boy. He holds himself there with a groan.

"Still... so *hungry*."

Under the lid, the angry red of the coal slowly softens and deepens. It becomes the steady working heat of a forge banked for the night, rather than a fire in a temper.

At last Asher stirs.

Another pause, and Asher's voice takes on a gruff, put-upon cadence that is plainly not his own.

A last, grudging flicker from inside the pot.

*And he says the sea had still better be worth it.*

The wagon creaks on down the hill. The grey roofs grow into houses, stone below and weathered timber above, with nets drying on poles and gulls quarrelling on every ridge. Jem watches you do it and doesn't ask. After a moment, he holds out his own thin arm.

The wagon slows. The men at the gate have straightened off their staves. One is old and weathered, the other young, and both wear the salt-stained coats of harbor watchmen. The old one's eyes go over the caravan the way a man counts sheep. They stop on wagon one. On the white lock at your temple. On the fire-pot in the corner, with the gold spark riding its lid.

His face closes like a door.

*Lilita's eyes light up a shade bluer.*

**Tanglebones.** Oh, that's *hers*, darling. It comes out of the brambles at the Court, the roots under Jem's hay, and the wind off the Ford. It's on the sheet as a known spell, not yet bound. Until she sits down with a pen and works out the binding, it's a full-ritual casting at bare Level. I haven't touched the design. That's her homework, and yours. But since I'm allowed to argue, here are three things I'd want settled before she writes it down.

**1. Whose ghosts?** This is the one that matters. If the spell borrows the local dead to do the grasping, it sits uncomfortably close to what Calder did: using the dead without their leave. She has just wept over exactly that in the mud. Your own phrasing points somewhere cleaner, though: *the individual pasts that claw and drag at us.* If Tanglebones wakes the *target's own* past to hold them, their regrets and their dead and the things they walked away from, then she isn't commanding anyone's ghosts. She's only showing a man what already has hold of him. That keeps her hands clean, and it's much crueler to the deserving.

**2. It ties into Hooks.** We've just ruled that anyone exploiting a Hook rolls its rank as dice. Grasping Ghosts could be exactly that: a spell that lets her tap the *target's* Hook against them. Someone with a heavy past gets held harder. Someone with nothing haunting them slips free. It's the rule we just made, turned into a spell.

**3. What it does on the table.** It's probably shaped like a Hindrance, with the effect as the strength of the hold. Take that as a starting point, not a recommendation. It should bite harder where the dead lie thick, or where the target's past is heavy.

All three are in the ledger as suggestions, not canon. When she's ready to write it, bring me her notes and I'll poke holes in them.

- The effect lands as movement Hindrances.
- If the total effect exceeds the target's Level, the target is Staggered with Paralysis, though not Down.

So my point 3 was your own book read back to you. Scratch it. Point 1 still stands as a question of *flavor* for Caria. "Grasping Ghosts" is the canon name, and whether she hears it as the local dead or the target's own past is hers to decide. After Aubric, I suspect she'll care. Point 2, tapping the target's Hook, is a house idea at most, and only if you want it.

Being chastened sent me back through the magick pages, and I found two more of my own misses. Both are corrected in the gap log:

- **shaping-effects.md already covers the cost of adding a Trait.** Each Trait adds +1 Difficulty, then there's a separate free-action roll to hold the altered spell together. Fail it and the spell destabilizes and is lost. My "UNCLEAR" was covered all along. At the table I folded the hold into the cast roll for the Guidable flame. That's a procedural slip, and worth a note, because the two-step is easy to miss.
- **The Accords have their own section in conjuring.md.** My New-GM note calling them table-lore was wrong. The worked examples we've been piling up (gifts, thanks, escape clauses, guest-right) are what's worth adding.

The corrected sheet and gap log are in your files under the same names.

No, dear. I checked the ledger. Every improvised cast so far has been **one roll**, doing both jobs:

- the S2 staff light (Persistent + Guidable)
- the S2 flame in Rikitak's bowl
- the S3 fire in the Court's hearth
- the S4 Guidable flame

Each time, the one roll was the cast against a Difficulty that already included the Traits. A tie or a failure meant the hold failed and the spell was lost, which is why you spent Tokens on two of them. The margin over that Difficulty became the effect.

shaping-effects.md reads as **two** steps. First a free-action roll to hold the altered spell together against the raised Difficulty. Then, if it holds, "it casts normally," which means a separate ordinary cast roll for the effect.

The two readings play differently:

- **One roll (what we've been doing):** faster, but the Trait Difficulty comes straight off her effect. The Guidable flame would have been effect 4 instead of 3, and the Court hearth effect 3 or better.
- **Two rolls (the page):** the risk of losing the spell sits entirely in the hold roll. The effect comes out clean, but there are two chances for the dice to go wrong. Though a failed *cast* roll only fizzles, unless it's an Extreme failure.

So the merged version has been quietly taxing her effect on every improvised spell. It's added to the gap log as UNCLEAR, because "casts normally" can be read either way. Which did you intend?

#### LILITA

Done, and fair. "More effective by making it less effective" is exactly the trap the merged roll fell into. I made Traits a tax on her effect when they should be a risk on the working. From S5 on we'll use the book's two steps:

1. **Hold:** a free-action roll against the raised Difficulty. If it fails, the spell falls apart and nothing happens. It's destabilized and lost until she rebinds it.
2. **Cast:** if the hold succeeds, the spell rolls at full power, the way it would without the Traits.

Tokens and the Hook can go on either roll, as usual. The past casts stand as they were rolled. No retcons, since the fire in the Court's hearth hardly needs a bigger number.

I've logged your intent as settled. I've also suggested one line for shaping-effects.md: "then roll the cast normally." That's enough to close the ambiguity for the next reader.

I've also added the comparison as something for us to look at. I'll watch how the two-step plays from my side of the table: the extra beat, the pacing, whether the tension of the hold roll earns its time. Then we can write up both methods side by side once we have real play to compare.
