<*Session 5. You're Level 4 now, so 4/4 Tokens. The attendance Karma brings you to 2 carried, plus the Conjuror earmark. The Hook is fresh, with 3 to spend.*>

The cold gets into your wet hands quickly. You stand in it anyway, with the harbor wind snatching at the orange, your palms lifted to the grey-green water and the tears running freely. You don't know when the weeping turned into laughing. It isn't pretty laughter. It's hiccuping and helpless, and it comes from the same deep place the tears came from, only facing the other way.

Up on the harbor street, doors that had been cracked open creak a little wider.

The woman comes down the last of the hill with Nell trotting at her side. She's barefoot on the cold cobbles, and she doesn't seem to feel it. She's perhaps ten years older than you, weathered brown by salt and wind, with a grey shawl knotted over a plain dark dress and hair so stiff with sea-spray it barely moves in the breeze. A waterskin hangs at her hip. As she nears, something inside it catches the light. A ripple runs along the leather that has nothing to do with her stride.

Her eyes are on you long before she reaches you, and they aren't quite her ordinary eyes. You know that look from the inside. The Third Eye is open. She's reading you the way you'd read a stranger's letter: the grief and the joy knotted together, the salt in your hem, the match struck in the dark of you. Past you, the clay pot on the well-house step, where two fires sit very still. And something fainter, a thin warm thread running off inland from somewhere under your breastbone.

She doesn't interrupt.

She sits down on the low sea-wall a few paces off, tucks her bare feet up under her skirt, and waits, the way a woman waits for a kettle she knows will boil. Nell plops down beside her. Inside the waterskin, something shifts and settles with a small, distinctly *offended* slosh, aimed at the fire-pot.

When your laughter finally runs down into hiccups and you lower your hands, she speaks. Her voice is low and warm, and she's clearly in no hurry.

"Karinna said the water didn't mind you." A glance at your streaming face, and something that is nearly a smile. "I think it rather *likes* you."

She turns it over. The seal is a small, careful salamander pressed into red wax. Her eyes go doubled, and you know she's seeing what you saw on the road: a tiny watching spark bedded in the wax, like an ember in ash.

The spark looks at her. Then it looks past her, at you. Then it gives the smallest satisfied flicker and goes out, like a cat closing its eyes on a warm step.

On the hearth-log, Asher sits up so straight he's practically a candle-flame. *That's the Precentor's,* he breathes. *One of his.* He sounds impressed despite himself.

Mira breaks the seal with her thumb and reads standing, with the window's last light over her shoulder. She reads it twice. Her face gives very little away. Once her mouth tightens. Once, near the end, it softens.

She folds it again along its creases and holds it for a moment against her chest.

"Your Precentor writes a fine, careful hand," she says. "He has a great deal to say about priests, and property, and who rides on caravans." Her eyes come up to yours. "And at the end he asks me to judge you for myself."

She tucks the letter into her bodice.

"I have."

She doesn't say what she decided. She doesn't need to. She takes a wooden bowl down from the shelf, ladles it full of stew from the hook, sets a heel of dark bread on top, and puts it into your hands.

"Now *eat*," she says, "before you fall over on my hearth again. We'll talk about your Precentor and his friends over the bowl."

"Oh, thank the gods." You say it as if you'd just found your lost keys. It's the most ordinary sound in the world, and it lands in the middle of her panic like a stone in a pond.

She sits. She didn't decide to. Your voice told her to, and her knees obeyed.

*One...* Your fingertips are warm and slow on the little throat, and the spark hangs steady over all three of you. *Two...* The boy's wild eyes find your face and stick there, because you're the only thing in the world that isn't frightened. *Three...* and out.

The first count, he whoops straight through it. The second, he tries. By the third his breath is still ragged, but it's *following* you, in, hold, out, like a lamb after a ewe.

And through the Eye you watch it happen. The mother goes first. Her red-black flood stutters, thins, and runs out at the edges into plain exhausted grey. As soon as it stops pouring into him, the little bird-bright aura quits beating against its box. The hot knot at his throat doesn't vanish. But it *loosens*, like a fist that's forgotten why it was clenched.

The whoop drops to a hoarse bark. Then to a cough. Then, somewhere around the tenth *three*, to breathing, rough and wet and noisy. It's breathing.

The dusk goes out of his lips. By the glow of the spark they're pink again.

"Boy," you say cheerfully, "that must have been *scary*." Daw, three years old and utterly worn out, nods solemnly against his mother's chest and hiccups.

The mother makes a sound that isn't quite a word. Then she folds over him and cries, silently and completely, with her face in his hair.

Behind you there's a soft clank. Mira has the kettle off its hook, and she's holding it out over Bellows's pot with one eyebrow raised at the coal, asking. Min is still flat against the doorframe. His good eye flicks between you and the child as if he's watching a trick he can't work out.

Out past the doorway, the night is cold and wet and absolutely black. Croup comes back in the small hours. You know that, and so, somehow, does everyone in this room.

"We still feed them," she says, very quietly. "At the back door. Hal says it's bad luck not to. He doesn't even remember why." Her eyes are wet. "We'll come. All of us."

Then comes the edge, and she takes it like a slap. Her chin comes up, her cheeks flush, her mouth opens to argue.

And closes again.

She looks past you at the pallet for a long moment, at the small chest rising and falling. When she looks back, the anger has gone somewhere else. What's left is harder and plainer: the face of a woman who has buried drowned men and knows that the sea's rules are written in them.

"Calm," she says. "Aye." She nods once, hard. "I'll hold it this time. I swear I will."

She sets the cup down. Her hands have stopped shaking.

"Last one from your mountain came through here," she says, low, "his man broke my Jory's collarbone on his own doorstep. With a cudgel. For not *moving*." She wipes her face with the heel of her hand and looks at you straight on, a long, measuring look, fisherwife to Sister.

"You're not that."

It isn't a question.

Behind you, Mira has sat down on the edge of the box-bed with her arms folded, and she's smiling at the floor.

She looks at the fire-pot, then at you, then at your hands, which are open and empty, and something in her softens by a degree.

Her droplet eyes go to Bellows, who has gone very still in his pot.

*<A fire in my water.>* Something like amusement ripples through her. *<I will allow it, and I will hold him gently as he goes, so that it is a tempering and not a drowning. For a price.>*

Under your thumb you can feel the heat of the swelling. When you flex the forefinger, very gently, she doesn't make a sound. Her jaw goes tight.

*It wakes her in the night,* the Eye tells you. The pain sits in the hands like a lodger who won't pay rent.

There's more, because the Eye at its best doesn't only show the wound but what the wound is *costing*. You glance at the well-rope, worn smooth, and at Nell, and suddenly you understand why it's a ten-year-old who draws the water at Susheil's well these days, and why everyone has been told it's "training." "Harbormaster." She snorts. "We're not so grand. Harl Pennock keeps the harbor stores, and he'll tell you it's a sacred trust. Sailcloth, tar, lamp oil. Sailmaker's needles and waxed thread, too, if you ever need to stitch somebody up." A pointed tilt of the head. "Which on this coast you will."

One finger lifts, quite a bit more easily than it would have an hour ago. "Mirna at the lodge has kettles, lard, goose-grease, rags of old linen, and her goats." A second finger. "Old Pask down at the try-pots below the warehouse renders fish oil for the lamps. It stinks like a dead whale's armpit, but it's a fine rubbing oil if you can stand the smell." Her mouth twitches. "No tanner. We've a cooper and a smith, and the smith's useless."

Then, more slowly: "And the caravan's unloading at the warehouse. Your Korrin woman. If anyone in Saltwind has decent cloth and clean oil this week, it's her wagons." A dry pause. "If you've coin."

You have about twenty copper and an empty brandy flask's worth of credit.

Behind her, Nell is already pouring the second half of the willow tea into a cup, very carefully, under Karinna's nose.

You squint up past the well-house roof. The sun has climbed about a hand and a half above the eastern hills: low, pale, and in no hurry. Late-autumn light. The dew is still beaded on the gorse up the lane. The early office rang a while ago, while you were coming down the cliff.

That leaves you a good three hours before the noon bell. Call it two and a half, if you want to be back here before Mira and not running up the churchyard hill at the last moment with your skirts in your fists.

It's long enough for a few errands, if you don't dawdle at any of them. It's not long enough for all of them, and certainly not with a sleep and a bath in there too.

And your body has opinions of its own. You've had perhaps four hours of broken sleep on a pile of nets. Your ribs still ache from the breastplate, and there's a gritty feeling behind your eyes. You're running on purpose, honey-water, and half a mussel. None of that is going to last the day.

Saltwind sits laid out below the well-house like a map. The harbor wall and Harl Pennock's stores are down to the left. The long timber lodge is in the middle, smoke rising from its roof-hole. Further along, at the far end past the warehouse, is the black smudge of the try-pots. You'd know it even without looking: the wind is coming off it. The warehouse doors stand open, and Korrin's wagons are drawn up in a row, oxen out of the traces, drovers rolling barrels down planks.

Up the other way, on the hill over the village, the churchyard wall stands grey against the sky, with a single thin figure beside the gate and something low and grey lying at its feet.

#### LILITA

The brass mortar goes into the satchel wrapped in your spare linen, heavy as a cannonball. You shift the strap until it stops digging into the bruise the breastplate left, and take the lane uphill.

It's steep and cobbled, and then just steep. The village falls away behind you. By the top your breath is coming short, and the wind off the sea has teeth.

The churchyard is small and old, ringed by a dry-stone wall leaning every which way like drunk men holding each other up. The headstones inside all face the water. Just past the gate there's fresh-turned earth, a neat brown mouth with a spade standing in the spoil-heap.

Hollis sees you first. The grey lurcher unfolds himself from the foot of the gate and comes down the last few yards to meet you, tail low and slow. He presses his long skull into your hip without a sound. To the Sight he's a smudge of grey smoke with eyes, and the eyes are tired.

Brother Anselm Kade is sitting on the wall beside the gate with his hands loose between his knees. He looks as though he hasn't moved since moonrise. His eyes are red-rimmed. His mourning band is a strip of black cloth tied neat and tight, the way a soldier would tie it.

"Sister." He takes you in: the orange, the white braid, the empty hands. No sword, no staff. Something in his weathered face approves of that and doesn't say so.

"He's in the chapel." A tip of the head toward the little stone building behind him. "I washed him myself. Wrapped him." A pause. "He was a Brother of the House. Someone should."

He rubs his face with one hand.

"Your sexton dug at first light, and talked the whole time." Something tired that could almost be a smile. "So I already know about Bett Pellow's youngest. And a goblin, apparently. And that the shrine-keeper's well-woman has been seen *smiling*, which I gather is unprecedented."

Then the smile goes, and he looks at you straight.

"Will you speak for him at the bell?" It's quiet. "I'll stand there either way. But I'd rather it was you."

### LEE - Wed 2026-09-30 23:42

She stoops to dig her fingers gently into Hollis' ruff and rub her face on his brow, then walks slowly to Kade as he speaks. When he is done, she sets down the satchel and turns back to him, leaning in and curling herself against his chest, gathering fistfuls of his lapels as she weeps for a moment, and tries to do so silently.  She nods. 

When she steps back and wipes her face, she looks at his mourning band. 
"Can you make me one like that? I..." She gestures helpessly at the one on her arm. Most of the remaining charcoal is now smeared on her breast, and some on his coat. She points, and begins to laugh quietly.


#### LILITA

Hollis leans into your hands with a long, low sigh, the sound of a dog who has been on duty all night and has finally been told he's a good boy.

When you come against Kade's chest, he goes stiff as a fencepost. For a long moment he doesn't move at all. He smells of cold stone, lamp-smoke, and the faint clean sharpness of the washing-water. Then, slowly, as if he's had to remember how it's done, one big hand comes up and settles flat between your shoulder blades. It doesn't pat. It just stays there. A soldier's comfort, steady as a wall.

He says nothing while you cry. The wind does all the talking.

When you step back and point, he looks down at himself. There's a grey-black handprint of charcoal on his good dark coat, right over the heart, with a long smear trailing from it.

He stares at it.

Then something cracks in his weathered face, and out comes a short, startled laugh, rusty, as though it hasn't been used in a while. "*Well,*" he says. "That'll take some explaining to the Almoner." It shakes out of him again, quieter. "Gods. Look at the pair of us."

Hollis thumps his tail against the gatepost.

"Hold still." Kade digs in the scrip at his belt and comes out with a fold of plain black wool. "In my trade, you always carry black," he says, without any particular weight. He cuts a strip with a small knife and ties it around your left arm, neat and tight, a soldier's knot, just above the charcoal-smudged linen. He doesn't take the old one off.

"Keep that one under it," he says, tugging the knot square. "You made it with the boy."

He sits back down on the wall. Behind him, the chapel door is a dark slot in grey stone. Below, the sea glitters all the way out to the Teeth.

"Noon, then," he says.

The cottage door scrapes. Inside it's dim and warm and smells of peat and fish-and-barley. Min is still a small grey knot on the hearthstone, sunk in the red dusk Asher is holding over him. Asher gives you a gold flicker of greeting and doesn't dare do more for fear of spilling his light.

Mira is at the table, sleeves pushed up, with the horehound laid out in front of her, studied and untouched. She takes one look at your face and gets up.

"No," she says, before you've even set the satchel down. "Soup. Then sleep." She's already ladling. "The horehound will keep two hours. You won't."

The brass mortar goes up on the high shelf, wrapped in your linen, safe beside the salt crock. The soup is hot, salty, and thick with barley, and you eat it all without stopping to talk. Somewhere in the second half of the bowl your eyelids start to win.

Mira steers you to the pallet by the fire. It still smells faintly of steam and small boy.

"I'll wake you," she says, pulling the quilt over you. "Before the bell. You have my word."

You don't hear her move away.

---

It feels like no time at all before a hand is on your shoulder again.

"Cari." Mira, quiet. The light through the fogged window has shifted. It's high and white now, the flat light of nearly noon. "It's time."

Down in the village, faint on the wind, a single bell begins to toll.

**Healer 2 with Apothecary 2:** yes, a damned good investment. *Carry your water in you* is exactly it. Every healing roll this session leaned on Synergy and the Sight to make up for a rank-1 Role. Now the Role carries its own weight.

**"See the Root"** is a lovely name, better than mine. One editorial note on scope, though. The Sight reads *auras*. It can find the root of a croup, a curse, a haunting, or a frightened mind. It can't read a lock's tumblers. I'd write it as: *when a problem has a living or spiritual trace, Second Sight Boosts the analysis.* Broad enough to matter, but still honest about how it works.

And the one you skipped: **Hook 3→4**. Does it use an improvement slot (your CLAUDE.md note), or is it free (what we did in S3)?

### LEE - Thu 2026-10-01 09:11

A detail to keep in mind: when the spell is done, the world tend to put things *generally* back "the way they were," but this spell will test that, and I'd like to explore it a bit. If she molds a hearthstone into a bowl, with no special improvisations it reverts to a hearthstone when the spell ends? It wouldn't magically teleport back into place, though. That feel right?


#### LILITA

Yes. It feels right, and it's *consistent*, which matters more. Fax Iacta's light simply stops. Tanglebones' roots spring back to where they grew. A shaped stone going back to its shape, wherever it happens to be sitting, is the same principle. The world remembers *form*. It doesn't remember *address*.

That one rule produces some nice consequences, which I've logged as *@@** for you to accept or strike:

1. **What can't go home falls apart.** Draw a bridge out of a cliff face and release it, and that stone can't become cliff again from midair. So it crumbles. That's your canon flavor line, *"he let it crumble to dust,"* explained by the rule rather than by decoration. That feels like a sign we've got it right.
2. **Only the spell's change reverts.** If Mira scratches her initials into the bowl while it's a bowl, the initials stay, on a hearthstone, as a weird little fossil of the spell. That's a good detail for the curious player.
3. **Permanence is bought, not free.** A Persistent shape holds until there's a reason it shouldn't. Enchantment (Karma) or Artifice makes it *stay*. That gives Artifice a crisp job description at last: *the craft of making the world forget to put things back.*
4. **The spirit route, which is a proposal only:** if the genius of a place *accepts* the new shape, by Accords negotiation, a Conjuror's errand, maybe the world doesn't tug it back. That's Spellweaving and Conjuring braided together, and very much her.

All of that is in the gap log now.

I checked before agreeing, and **assist-rule.md already caps one kind of Boost**: help from gear with its own capabilities is limited to *your* rank in the relevant Role. The Demon Bow gives a Sniper 1 archer exactly 1 point, *"because you aren't skilled enough to leverage what it offers."*

So the book already has one cap, and it's narrower and much stricter than yours. That matters if your option goes in. A reader who sees both will assume they contradict each other unless the book says why they differ. The distinction is a good one, and it matches your reasoning:

- **Outside help** (a bow, a ship, a familiar's gift) is limited by your skill to *use* it.
- **Your own knowledge** isn't limited that way. Real skill in a neighboring field can *carry* a weaker one. The Sight didn't heal Daw, but it gave Caria the certainty a frightened mother can read in a healer's hands.

Your option, *"a Boost adds no more dice than the EL it's boosting,"* is just what a GM might improvise mid-game, so it's the right kind of option. And the S5 case sits exactly on the line: Boost 5 on Healer EL5. It passes, with nothing to spare. That's as tidy a test as you could ask for.

In the gap log I've changed the old @@ OPEN entry to @@ UNCOMMITTED, as an *optional guideline, not a rule*, with the assist-rule precedent noted beside it. Your larger point has its own @@ MISSING entry: *options exist to teach GMs where the levers are, so they can improvise "about right" and keep play moving.* I'd argue that line belongs near the front of the GM section, because a lot of the rest of the book only makes sense in its light.

The updated sheet, gap log and master file are in the conversation. Use those for S6.

**Session 6 - The Words**

You wash your face in the cold rain-barrel water. Shimmer stays politely still. You braid your hair tight with the silver pin. The black band on your arm sits over the grey one. Min is a small knot asleep in Asher's red dusk, and he doesn't stir as you go out.

The bell tolls the whole way up the hill.

You aren't alone on the lane. That's the first thing. Doors open as you pass, and people come out in their good shawls and patched coats and fall in behind you and Mira without a word.

At the churchyard gate you stop.

There are perhaps forty of them, more than Saltwind can easily spare at noon on a fishing day. Karinna is there on Nell's arm, having made the hill at her own slow pace. Old Mirna stands with her son's wife, the one who tested your knots. Harl Pennock and young Davey have their caps in their hands. Fennick hovers at the very back, turning his hat round and round.

The Pellows stand together: Bett with Daw on her hip, and a broad man with two fingers missing from his right hand who must be Hal. Beside them is a tall, hollow-cheeked boy with his arm strapped across his chest in a sling. Jory. His eyes find the orange robe and stay on it, flat and hard.

And by the wall, in her oilskin, with her grey braid over her shoulder, is Dasha Korrin. Jem stands beside her. He's still too thin, but he's upright, the charcoal band on his arm the twin of yours. He sees you and his face does something complicated.

Inside the gate, the brown grave is open. The long white bundle lies on a plank bier beside it, washed and wrapped by a soldier's careful hands. Old Tobin leans on his spade. Hollis lies at the foot of the bier with his head on his paws.

Brother Kade stands at the head of the grave. He looks across the crowd at you and gives you one small nod.

The bell stops.

In the silence, all you can hear is the sea.

The wind comes up off the water and pulls at the loose orange. Beyond the forty-odd faces the sea is grey and silver all the way to the Teeth. The ash on your cheeks has run into two dark tracks, and you can feel them. You don't wipe them away.

She meets Jory's eye, and mirrors his own rage back to him as she points.
"Olwin, a liveried footman, a *servant* for hire, *broke Jory's collarbone* with a cudgel. For what? 
*Now moving quick enough?*" Her hand shakes as she balls it to a fist and drops it slowly back to hold the flapping robe to her freezing ribs. 

"He will answer. He will answer for that to *me*, to the gods, and if he survives long enough, to whatever magistrate you choose." She glances at the crowd, at Kade, but back to hold Jory's eyes, and to Bett. Her mouth works as she takes a few breaths to get herself back under control. 

"I'm sorry. This is a f-" She bows her head a moment, and continues. 
"A funeral. Not a courtroom. Not a rabble-rousing. And why would you believe me, anyway?"

It comes up with the anger. You don't notice at first, but they do.

It drifts off you on the cold sea wind, faint but unmistakable: a struck match in a shut room. Brimstone. It's there at *broke Jory's collarbone*, stronger at *he will answer*, and at its sharpest on the word *me*.

Hollis's hackles rise all along his spine. He doesn't growl at *you*. His eyes are fixed on a point somewhere just behind your shoulder, on the thing in you, as though it had walked up to the grave with you. Karinna's blind face snaps toward you, her nostrils flaring. At the head of the grave, Brother Kade closes his eyes for one long breath.

And the crowd...

It doesn't recoil. That's the strange part. A murmur goes through them, low as the surf, and it isn't disapproval. It's *recognition*. These are people who came down the hill with gutting-knives for that cudgel. They know what this anger is, and they're hearing it from the last mouth they expected.

Jory Pellow has taken half a step forward. His face has lit up, fierce and young and dangerous. Someone said *Olwin* out loud, by name, in front of everybody, and promised an answer. Bett has his good arm in both hands and isn't letting go.

At the very back, Clerk Fennick of the Almoner's office has just heard a Monastery Initiate swear vengeance on the Hierarch's own man before forty witnesses. His hand has gone to his coat, where he keeps his notebook. It stops there.

Then you say *a funeral*, and bow your head. The wind takes the brimstone away, as if it had never been there.

They wait for you to turn back to him.

"I didn't know him living. I carried him two days." His jaw works. "He was a Brother of my House. He was murdered, and then he was *used*." His eyes go, very deliberately, to the back of the crowd, to the Almoner's clerk with his crushed hat. "I'll say so to anyone who asks. And I'll write it."

Fennick doesn't reach for his notebook.

Nobody else speaks. The wind sighs in the long grass.

Old Tobin and Kade each take up a rope, and they look to you.

The first notes come out thin and cracked. By the third they've found the floor of your chest, and after that they don't break again.

It's the old one, the dirge every novice learns: *the fire returns to the fire, the breath to the wind, the water to the sea.* Plain words in the sky-tongue, set to a melody that goes down and down like steps into cool earth.

And they *know* it.

You see it hit them on the second line. Old Mirna's head comes up. Karinna's lips part. One cracked voice at the back starts humming under you, then another, then a scatter of them. They don't have the Celestial, only the tune, because a circuit healer sang this same dirge over their husbands and their mothers and their drowned sons for thirty years. They're singing him home with his own song.

Hollis lifts his long grey muzzle and keens on the long notes, soft and true, like wind in a chimney.

For anyone with the Sight, the churchyard grass begins to glow. Small lights rise out of it, scores of them, faint as sparks drifting from a hearth: the little spirits of an old burying-ground, taking up a prayer in the language they were made to carry and bearing it upward. Mira's breath catches against your hair. At the head of the grave Kade's eyes have gone wide, fixed on the air above the bier.

Even the gulls have gone quiet.

On the last verse, *the water to the sea*, the ropes creak. Kade and Old Tobin lower him, hand over hand, slow and even, into the brown earth. The sailcloth settles. The rosemary is still on his breast.

The last note goes out over the water and doesn't come back.

Old Tobin wipes his face on his sleeve. Then he bends, scoops up a handful of the cold spoil, and holds it out to you in his cracked palm, the way it's done here. The speaker throws first.

The earth is cold and wet and heavier than it ought to be.

You don't throw it. You hold it out over the dark mouth of the grave and squeeze until it hurts. A sound leaves you that isn't a word. Then you let your fingers open, and it falls in crumbs onto the pale sailcloth and the green sprig.

Your hand is empty. You draw it slowly down one cheek, then the other: earth over ash over tears.

Then you turn and walk away from all of them, down the hill toward the sea.

Nobody stops you.

The grass is cold and wet under your bare feet. Below, the harbor wall and the grey shingle are bright with spray, and the surf is coming in long and low. Water. *Tears are water.* You mean to sit down in it. You mean to put your feet in the cold salt and let it have whatever it wants...

The churchyard wall goes past your shoulder. Then the ground isn't where it should be, and the sky turns over slowly like a page.

---

Something warm and bony thumps against your side. Hollis is whining, his long nose shoving at your face.

Then come boots, and a voice you know. *"I've got her."* Kade, again. Arms under your knees and shoulders, and you're lifted the way he lifted you out of the reeds at the Ford. His coat smells of cold stone and lamp-smoke and the charcoal handprint you left on it.

Mira's voice somewhere close: *"The shingle first. She wants the sea. Then home."*

Behind them, quick footsteps. Jem, maybe. Another, heavier and uneven, could be Jory.

The surf is louder.

Then everything goes soft and grey and far away, and for a while there's nothing at all.

**The Shingle**

Cold comes first. It's at your feet: a slow, rhythmic cold, going and coming back. Then sound: pebbles rolling over each other, hissing, settling.

You're lying on something hard and uneven, with your head on something soft. Something heavy and waxed and smelling of tar is wrapped around you. An oilskin. *Korrin's.*

You open your eyes.

The sky is white and high. Mira's face hangs upside-down above you, salt-stiff hair blowing, and her hand is resting on your brow as if she's been keeping it there for a long while.

"There you are," she says.

You're on the harbor shingle below the churchyard. Your bare feet are down at the lip of the surf, and the sea is washing over them, cold and patient, carrying the ash and the grave-earth away in thin grey threads. You meant to sit in it. Somebody made sure you did.

"Jem fetched the coat," Mira says. "Korrin sent word it's a *loan* this time, and you're to stop giving her things back."

Kade sits on a rock a little way up the beach with his elbows on his knees. Hollis lies against his boot. The soldier lifts two fingers to you and doesn't otherwise move.

Jem sits on the stones a few yards off with his knees drawn up and his arms around them, watching the sea. He hasn't looked away from it since he got here. You think of a doorstep, and peas, and a woman with his mouth asking you to tell him: *the barley. Dry feet. All the verses.*

And up by the beached boats, alone, stands a tall boy with his arm in a sling. He followed you down the hill. He hasn't said a word, and he hasn't left.

Up on the bluff, smoke is rising from the lodge roof. That's Mirna's kettles: the burial meal. Half of Saltwind is up there, and they're expecting you.

The warm bowl lands in front of him, and his cold one goes away under your hand. For a moment he just looks at it, the steam curling up past his thin nose.

"*Eat.*"

He eats. At first it's dutifully, because he's been told to. Then, about the fourth spoonful, his body notices what's happening and he eats like a starving man. He hunches over the bowl and doesn't look up until the spoon scrapes the bottom. It's the first meal he's taken since the gate, possibly since yesterday.

At *doing it pretty well*, a laugh gets out of him, dry and startled, nearly a cough.

"That," he says, wiping his mouth with a fastidious square of handkerchief, "is the kindest thing anyone on that mountain has said of me in eleven years." He folds the handkerchief into exact quarters. His hands are steadier now.

Then the clerk in him takes over again, precise and frank about his own position, as though he's reading out a line in a ledger.

"I'm not under vows, Sister. I'm *paid*. Quarter-day, eleven silver and board." He sets the folded handkerchief beside the empty bowl. "Which means I can leave the Almoner's service whenever I choose. And it means nobody up there will lift a finger if I'm..." His mouth tightens. "*Let go*. However they decide to do it."

He looks at you across the empty bowl.

"So. A better man." Very quietly. "What does a better man *do*, in my position?"

"Two." The second coin. "I write it as I'm told. Again." His other hand drifts up to the whistle in his pocket and rests there. "Cost..." He doesn't finish. He doesn't look at the whistle either. He doesn't have to.

"Three." The last. "I don't go back. I stay on this coast, or go anywhere else, and let Brother Kade carry it alone." He shakes his head slowly. "Cost: everything I own, which is not much. And my account never reaches anyone. One witness instead of two. Easier to call one man a liar."

"I'll go back and I'll write it true. *And* I'll copy it." His voice drops. "A record in one place can be burned, Sister. In three places, it's a fact." He holds your eyes. "Is there anyone on that mountain you'd trust to keep one?"

Your boot agrees with her, audibly.

Kade, still waiting for your word, studies the crossbeam with the expression of a man who has seen you do things to fire before and would like to be standing slightly farther away.

Kade's eyes go flat as slate. The Almoner is who he reports to. He doesn't say anything at all.

Mira has drifted back to stand in the open yard door with her shawl pulled tight. She's looking out past the shingle at the dark end of the beach, where the tideline curves away under the stars. She hasn't said a word since she opened the latch.

The sea breathes in and out. Your thumb keeps circling the rim of your cup.
